# Russian — Established Vocabulary/Grammar: Using Russian Vocabulary, Part 2 (Units 5 tail–13)

**Source:** Terence Wade, *Using Russian Vocabulary* (Cambridge University Press, 2009). PDF:
`source_reference/languages/Russian/Russian Learning Pack [up-to-date as of 2012]/Vol 1 of 3/
56.Using Russian Vocabulary.pdf`, PDF pages 216–430 (printed pp. ~204–418+), covering the tail end
of Unit 5 (Personal Characteristics — exercises only, one matching-exercise vocabulary set),
Unit 6 (Clothes, Footwear, and Accessories; Food and Drink) in full, and Units 7–13 in full or
substantially full. Unit 13 (Medicine, Health) is cut off mid-exercise at PDF page 430 and
continues in the sibling chunk covering pp. 431–636 (`established/021`); do not treat Unit 13 as
complete in this file.

**Coverage note.** This is a thematic vocabulary-building reference (not a graded grammar), organized
by topic (Unit → thematic sub-fields → Level 1/2/3 by difficulty), each level headed by short
categorized vocabulary lists followed by drill exercises. Per the coverage rule, every distinct
vocabulary item from the Level 1/2/3 lists is captured below; the drill exercises themselves
(matching exercises, fill-the-gap, translate-into-Russian, conjugation drills, etc.) are skipped
as they only recombine vocabulary already captured from the same unit's lists — **except** where an
exercise itself is the sole place a distinct new lexical item appears (Unit 5's synonym-matching
exercise introduces vocabulary not listed elsewhere in this chunk's range, so those items are
captured; several units' "Similes"/"Phrases" exercise sections introduce genuine idioms/proverbs not
otherwise listed, and a representative, non-exhaustive selection of these is captured with brief
glosses, since idiom drill lists run long and mostly recombine roots already tabulated).

Each unit's "Origins of words" / "Word formation" prose sections (etymological notes on loanwords,
calques, and word-formation patterns) are paraphrased into the Grammar points section below, grouped
per unit, rather than reproduced — this is exactly the analytical, non-verbatim treatment the
copyright-discipline rule calls for.

**PDF-extraction note.** This PDF has a genuine, clean Cyrillic text layer (no font-substitution
cipher detected — Cyrillic renders correctly and consistently; spot-checked against 20+ known
words such as блузка, брюки, галстук, рубашка, яблоко, которые decode identically whether read via
`pdftotext` raw mode or `-layout` mode). The one recurring artifact: this source prints an acute
stress-accent mark as a combining diacritic slightly offset from its vowel, and `pdftotext -layout`
sometimes reflows that stray accent glyph onto its own line or attaches it to the wrong adjacent
line when a vocabulary entry's headword wraps across lines (long entries with a parenthetical
genitive/conjugation gloss). The base Cyrillic lettering itself is never affected, only the accent
placement on wrapped multi-line entries — reconstructed by eye against the visible stress-mark
convention used consistently on single-line entries on the same page. No handwritten marginalia was
found anywhere in this range (this copy shows no reader annotation).

---
